## claude-haiku-4.5 k=10 target=955 score=84.0

**expected:** “That’s his hump. _There_, _there_, give it to him!” whispered Starbuck.

**got:** "That's his hump. There, there, it is!" whispered Starbuck. "On the left, on the left!—he comes this way!" and the three boats straggled in a line astern. Queequeg stood sightless; as if paused by some spell, he kept his harpoon loosely poised above his head, that he might precipitate its descent upon an assured sight. Aye, 'tis the hump! There—there—there again! "Do you see him?" cried Starbuck. "See him?" aye, as plainly as I see a white bone through black water. There was a ship-load of ivory in that hump, and a ship-load of oil in that body. He piled upon the whale's white hump the sum of all the rage and hate felt by his whole race from Adam down; and then, as if his chest had been a mortar, he burst his hot heart's shell upon it. The harpoon was hurled. With a short, sharp sound the pole of the harpoon snapped in the socket of the tub, and simultaneously, as the line ran out, a quick cry came from Queequeg—"Haul in!—haul in!" But the whale was not hauled in; instead, the boat was hauled in towards the whale; for, as the line tightened and strained under the boat's bow, goading it forward, the whale seemed pulling us after him in a strange sort of way.

**expected:** “Hark ye yet again—the little lower layer. All visible objects, man, are but as pasteboard masks. If man will strike, strike through the mask! How can the prisoner reach outside except by thrusting through the wall? To me, the white whale is that wall, shoved near to me. Sometimes I think there’s naught beyond. But ’tis enough. He tasks me; he heaps me; I see in him outrageous strength, with an inscrutable malice sinewing it. That inscrutable thing is chiefly what I hate; and be the white whale agent, or be the white whale principal, I will wreak that hate upon him. Talk not to me of blasphemy, man; I’d strike the sun if it insulted me. For could the sun do that, then could I do the other; since there is ever a sort of fair play herein, jealousy presiding over all creations. But not my master, man, is even that fair play. Who’s over me? Truth hath no confines. Take off thine eye! more intolerable than fiends’ glarings is a doltish stare! So, so; thou reddenest and palest; my heat has melted thee to anger-glow. But look ye, Starbuck, what is said in heat, that thing unsays itself. There are men from whom warm words are small indignity. I meant not to incense thee. Let it go. Look! see yonder Turkish cheeks of spotted tawn—living, breathing pictures painted by the sun. The Pagan leopards—the unrecking and unworshipping things, that live; and seek, and give no reasons for the torrid life they feel! The crew, man, the crew! Are they not one and all with Ahab, in this matter of the whale? See Stubb! he laughs! See yonder Chilian! he snorts to think of it. Stand up amid the general hurricane, thy one tost sapling cannot, Starbuck! And what is it? Reckon it. ’Tis but to help strike a fin; no wondrous feat for Starbuck. What is it more? From this one poor hunt, then, the best lance out of all Nantucket, surely he will not hang back, when every foremast-hand has clutched a whetstone? Ah! constrainings seize thee; I see! the billow lifts thee! Speak, but speak!—Aye, aye! thy silence, then, _that_ voices thee. **got:** "Hark ye yet again,—the little lower layer. All visible objects, man, are but as pasteboard masks. If man will strike, strike through the mask! How can the prisoner reach outside except by thrusting through the wall? To me, in this matter, it is far easier for a straw to swim against a stream than for any visible object to do so. That inscrutable thing is chiefly what I hate; and be the white whale agent, or be the white whale principal, I will wreak that hate upon him. Talk not to me of blasphemy, man; I'd strike the sun if it insulted me. ## claude-sonnet-4.6 k=3 target=1185 score=84.0

**expected:** Whatever superstitions the sperm whalemen in general have connected with the sight of this object, certain it is, that a glimpse of it being so very unusual, that circumstance has gone far to invest it with portentousness. So rarely is it beheld, that though one and all of them declare it to be the largest animated thing in the ocean, yet very few of them have any but the most vague ideas concerning its true nature and form; notwithstanding, they believe it to furnish to the sperm whale his only food. For though other species of whales find their food above water, and may be seen by man in the act of feeding, the spermaceti whale obtains his whole food in unknown zones below the surface; and only by inference is it that any one can tell of what, precisely, that food consists. At times, when closely pursued, he will disgorge what are supposed to be the detached arms of the squid; some of them thus exhibited exceeding twenty and thirty feet in length. They fancy that the monster to which these arms belonged ordinarily clings by them to the bed of the ocean; and that the sperm whale, unlike other species, is supplied with teeth in order to attack and tear it.

**got:** Whatever superstitions the sperm whalemen in general have connected with the sight of this object, certain it is, that a glimpse of it being so very unusual, that circumstance has gone far to invest it with portentousness. So rarely is it beheld, that though one and all of them declare it to be the largest animated thing in the ocean, yet very few of them have any but the most vague ideas concerning its true nature and form; notwithstanding, they believe it to furnish to the sperm whale his only food. For though other species of whales find their food above water, and may be seen by man in the act of feeding, the spermaceti whale obtains his whole food in unknown zones below the surface; and only by inference is it that any one can tell of what, precisely, that food consists. At any rate, when you watch those live crabs that nestle here on this bonnet, such an idea will be almost sure to occur to you; unless, indeed, your fancy has been fixed by the technical term "crown," also bestowed upon it; in which case you will take great interest in thinking how this mighty monster is actually a diademed king of the sea, whose green crown has been put together for him in those far Antilles.

But he rallies. “I seek a passage in this ship to Tarshish; how soon sail ye, sir?” Thus far the busy Captain had not looked up to Jonah, though the man now stands before him; but no sooner does he hear that hollow voice, than he darts a scrutinizing glance. “We sail with the next coming tide,” at last he slowly answered, still intently eyeing him. “No sooner, sir?”—“Soon enough for any honest man that goes a passenger.” Ha! ## kimi-k3 k=10 target=1734 score=84.0

**expected:** “What now?” said the Guernsey-man to Stubb.

**got:** “What now is the Guernsey-man to do?” thought Stubb, standing apart from the captain, and eyeing him with a look of profound concern. “He’s a queer sort of chap, and I must humor him. But I’ll tell him the truth, and no mistake.” So, turning to the Guernsey-man, he said, “Tell him that I think the whole affair is a humbug, and that he had better cut the whale adrift, and not bother himself any more about it.” The Guernsey-man, with a grave face, translated this into French, adding, however, a few embellishments of his own, to the effect that Stubb had seen many such whales, and that they were all alike worthless. The captain listened attentively, and then, with a shrug of his shoulders, replied that he was not to be so easily dissuaded from his purpose; that he had heard of the value of ambergris, and that he was determined to try his luck. Stubb, perceiving that the captain was obstinate, and that it was useless to argue with him, resolved to have a little fun at his expense. So, with a wink at the Guernsey-man, he said, “Tell him that I admire his taste in dress, and that I think his red velvet vest is the prettiest thing I have seen this voyage.” The Guernsey-man, with a suppressed smile, translated this into French, and the captain, highly pleased, bowed and smiled, and said that he was glad to find that the American officer had so good an eye for the beautiful. Stubb, encouraged by this success, went on to say, “Tell him that I think his whiskers and moustache are the most becoming I have ever seen, and that I should like to have a pair just like them.” The Guernsey-man translated this also, and the captain, still more pleased, stroked his whiskers, and said that he was flattered by the compliment. Stubb, seeing that the captain was now in a good humor, thought it was time to come to the point. So, with a serious face, he said, “Tell him that I have a great mind to buy his whale of him, and that I will give him a good price for it.” The Guernsey-man translated this, and the captain, surprised and delighted, asked what Stubb would give. Stubb, with a look of cunning, replied, “Tell him that I will give him a dollar a pound for all the ambergris he can find in the whale.” The Guernsey-man translated this, and the captain, overjoyed, said that he would accept the offer, and that he would set his men to work at once to extract the ambergris. Stubb, seeing that the captain was now completely taken in, could hardly contain his laughter. But, with a great effort, he kept a grave face, and said, “Tell him that I will send my boat for the ambergris to-morrow morning, and that I will pay him in cash.” The Guernsey-man translated this, and the captain, highly delighted, shook Stubb warmly by the hand, and said that he was a gentleman and a scholar, and that he was glad to have made his acquaintance. Stubb, with a bow and a smile, took his leave, and, descending into his boat, returned to the Pequod, highly amused at the success of his scheme.

**got:** It was curious and not unpleasing, how Peleg and Bildad were affected at this juncture, particularly Captain Bildad. For loath to depart, yet; very loath to leave, for good, a ship bound on so long and perilous a voyage—beyond both stormy Capes; a ship in which some thousands of his hard earned dollars were invested; a ship, in which an old comrade of his, a man nearly as old as he, once more embarked to encounter all the jaw of Leviathan; a ship that was going out to meet him with a cargo of precious life in her hold, whose welfare lay heavy on his conscience; poor old Bildad lingered long; paced the deck with anxious strides; ran down into the cabin to speak another farewell word there; again came on deck, and looked to windward; looked towards the land; looked aloft at the rigging; looked right and left; looked everywhere and nowhere; and at last, mechanically coiling a rope upon its pin, convulsively grasped stout Peleg by the hand, and holding up a lantern, for a moment stood gazing heroically in his face, as much as to say, "Nevertheless, friend Peleg, I can bear it; yes, I can."

I don't know where he gets 'em. But he's always at it; and when he comes back with the head, why then we know he's sold it, and so we know he's coming back. But I'll tell you what it is, my friend, he's a terrible man when he's going. He's a terrible man. He's got a lot of 'em."

As the line passed round and round the loggerhead; so also, just before reaching that point, it blisteringly passed through and through both of Stubb’s hands, from which the hand-cloths, or squares of quilted canvas sometimes worn at these times, had accidentally dropped. ## claude-haiku-4.5 k=10 target=1633 score=81.0

**expected:** But far beneath this wondrous world upon the surface, another and still stranger world met our eyes as we gazed over the side. For, suspended in those watery vaults, floated the forms of the nursing mothers of the whales, and those that by their enormous girth seemed shortly to become mothers. The lake, as I have hinted, was to a considerable depth exceedingly transparent; and as human infants while suckling will calmly and fixedly gaze away from the breast, as if leading two different lives at the time; and while yet drawing mortal nourishment, be still spiritually feasting upon some unearthly reminiscence;—even so did the young of these whales seem looking up towards us, but not at us, as if we were but a bit of Gulfweed in their new-born sight. Floating on their sides, the mothers also seemed quietly eyeing us. One of these little infants, that from certain queer tokens seemed hardly a day old, might have measured some fourteen feet in length, and some six feet in girth. He was a little frisky; though as yet his body seemed scarce yet recovered from that irksome position it had so lately occupied in the maternal reticule; where, tail to head, and all ready for the final spring, the unborn whale lies bent like a Tartar’s bow. The delicate side-fins, and the palms of his flukes, still freshly retained the plaited crumpled appearance of a baby’s ears newly arrived from foreign parts.

**got:** But far beneath this wondrous world upon the surface, another and still stranger world met our eyes as we gazed over the side. For, suspended in those watery vaults, floated the forms of the nursing mothers of the whales, and those that by their enormous girth seemed shortly to become mothers. The lake, as I have hinted, was to a considerable depth exceedingly transparent; and as human infants while suckling will calmly and fixedly gaze away from the breast, as if leading two different lives at the time; and while yet drawing mortal nourishment, be still spiritually feasting upon some unearthly reminiscence;—even so did the young of these whales seem looking up towards us, but not at us, as if we were but a bit of Gulf-weed in their new-born sight. Floating on their sides, the mothers also seemed quietly eyeing us. One of these little infants, that had lost its dam, affectionately clasped a rugged rock, seeming to seek support from it. As with its maternal ponderosity the great Leviathan darts so swiftly over the ocean, that the wakes he leaves behind look like the trails of three fleets; and as, behaving thus, this instant and mighty creature imbeds a tender love for its off-spring, unused to the vast and Patagonian proportions of its sire;—inexpressible tenderness was in every feature of these swarthy mothers, as over and over again they circled their spouts side-long against the flanks of their infants, as if caressing them, as a cow her calf. Even the last and least wrinkled of the whales, even the youngest calf of the pod, was larger and more formidable than the mightiest animated thing of the land. But the mother mingled with the rest, and seemed intent upon nothing but soothing and protecting the young.

But I am getting out of my reckoning. I keep etcetera-ing about, as it were, hap-hazard from billow to billow of my subject, and shall at last infallibly lead the reader away from his bearings in perilous seas of my own cruising, therefore I shall come about and point towards home.

**got:** But as the junior mates were hurrying to execute the order, a pale man, with a bandaged head, arrested them—Radney the chief mate. Ever since the blow, he had lain in his berth; but that morning, hearing the tumult on the deck, he had crept out, and thus far had watched the whole scene. Such was his state of mind, that all the added moodiness which always afterwards, to the very day of sailing in the Pequod on the present voyage, sat brooding on his brow. Nor is it so very unlikely, that far from distrusting his fitness for another whaling voyage, on account of such dark symptoms, the calculating people of that prudent isle were inclined to harbor the conceit, that for those very reasons he was all the better qualified and set on edge, for a pursuit so full of rage and wildness as the bloody hunt of whales. Gnawed within and scorched without, with the infixed, unrelenting fangs of some incurable idea; such an one, could he be found, would seem the very man to dart his iron and lift his lance against the most appalling of all brutes. Or, if for any reason thought to be corporeally incapacitated for that, yet such an one would seem superlatively competent to cheer and howl on his underlings to the attack. But be all this as it may, certain it is, that with the mad secret of his unabated rage bolted up and keyed in him, Ahab had purposely sailed upon the present voyage with the one only and all-engrossing object of hunting the White Whale. Had any one of his old acquaintances on shore but half dreamed of what was lurking in him then, how soon would their aghast and righteous souls have wrenched the ship from such a fiendish man! They were bent on profitable cruises, the profit to be counted down in dollars from the mint. He was intent on an audacious, immitigable, and supernatural revenge.

**got:** Now, one of the peculiar characteristics of the savage in his domestic hours, is his wonderful patience of industry. An ancient Hawaiian war-club or spear-paddle, in its full multiplicity and elaboration of carving, is as great a trophy of human perseverance as the pyramids; and till the military elephants of antiquity were in use, the war-canoe of the savages was not much inferior in point of workmanship to the war-chariot of the Romans. And though none of the whalemen have as yet attempted to rival the Egyptian pyramid in the matter of mere size, yet in point of elaborate carving, the Right Whale-bone, and other like skrimshander articles, are not unworthy of a place beside the finest work of the most accomplished European artists.

## deepseek-v4-pro k=3 target=1771 score=77.0

**expected:** But it so happened, that those boats, without seeing Pip, suddenly spying whales close to them on one side, turned, and gave chase; and Stubb’s boat was now so far away, and he and all his crew so intent upon his fish, that Pip’s ringed horizon began to expand around him miserably. By the merest chance the ship itself at last rescued him; but from that hour the little negro went about the deck an idiot; such, at least, they said he was. The sea had jeeringly kept his finite body up, but drowned the infinite of his soul. Not drowned entirely, though. Rather carried down alive to wondrous depths, where strange shapes of the unwarped primal world glided to and fro before his passive eyes; and the miser-merman, Wisdom, revealed his hoarded heaps; and among the joyous, heartless, ever-juvenile eternities, Pip saw the multitudinous, God-omnipresent, coral insects, that out of the firmament of waters heaved the colossal orbs. He saw God’s foot upon the treadle of the loom, and spoke it; and therefore his shipmates called him mad. So man’s insanity is heaven’s sense; and wandering from all mortal reason, man comes at last to that celestial thought, which, to reason, is absurd and frantic; and weal or woe, feels then uncompromised, indifferent as his God.

## claude-sonnet-4.6 k=10 target=814 score=76.0

**expected:** But there are other instances where this whiteness loses all that accessory and strange glory which invests it in the White Steed and Albatross.

**got:** But there are other instances where this whiteness loses all that accessory charm, and becomes a positive terror; and even in the case of those who have never beheld it, this quality of whiteness—this indefinite, half-attained, unimaginable sublimity about it that so much enhances the popular terror—is not without its analogy in the natural world. Consider the universal cannibalism of the sea; all whose creatures prey upon each other, carrying on eternal war since the world began. Consider all this; and then turn to this green, gentle, and most docile earth; consider them both, the sea and the land; and do you not find a strange analogy to something in yourself? For as this appalling ocean surrounds the verdant land, so in the soul of man there lies one insular Tahiti, full of peace and joy, but encompassed by all the horrors of the half known life. God keep thee! Push not off from that isle, thou canst never return!

## deepseek-v4-pro k=1 target=977 score=76.0

**expected:** Ahab well knew that although his friends at home would think little of his entering a boat in certain comparatively harmless vicissitudes of the chase, for the sake of being near the scene of action and giving his orders in person, yet for Captain Ahab to have a boat actually apportioned to him as a regular headsman in the hunt—above all for Captain Ahab to be supplied with five extra men, as that same boat’s crew, he well knew that such generous conceits never entered the heads of the owners of the Pequod. Therefore he had not solicited a boat’s crew from them, nor had he in any way hinted his desires on that head. Nevertheless he had taken private measures of his own touching all that matter. Until Cabaco’s published discovery, the sailors had little foreseen it, though to be sure when, after being a little while out of port, all hands had concluded the customary business of fitting the whaleboats for service; when some time after this Ahab was now and then found bestirring himself in the matter of making thole-pins with his own hands for what was thought to be one of the spare boats, and even solicitously cutting the small wooden skewers, which when the line is running out are pinned over the groove in the bow: when all this was observed in him, and particularly his solicitude in having an extra coat of sheathing in the bottom of the boat, as if to make it better withstand the pointed pressure of his ivory limb; and also the anxiety he evinced in exactly shaping the thigh board, or clumsy cleat, as it is sometimes called, the horizontal piece in the boat’s bow for bracing the knee against in darting or stabbing at the whale; when it was observed how often he stood up in that boat with his solitary knee fixed in the semi-circular depression in the cleat, and with the carpenter’s chisel gouged out a little here and straightened it a little there; all these things, I say, had awakened much interest and curiosity at the time. But almost everybody supposed that this particular preparative heedfulness in Ahab must only be with a view to the ultimate chase of Moby Dick; for he had already revealed his intention to hunt that mortal monster in person. But such a supposition did by no means involve the remotest suspicion as to any boat’s crew being assigned to that boat.

**got:** Ahab well knew that although his friends at home would think little of his entering a boat in certain comparatively harmless vicissitudes of the chase, for the sake of being near the scene of action and giving his orders in person, yet for Captain Ahab to have a boat, actually to descend into it as a harpooneer, his crippled state forbade that; and therefore his going in quest of the whale was an irregular, unprecedented, and, in fact, a purely spiritual and mystical act, which no merely mortal man could have dared to undertake.

**got:** And as for my exact knowledge of the bones of the leviathan in their gigantic, full grown development, for that rare knowledge I am indebted to my late royal friend Tranquo, king of Tranque, one of the Arsacides. For being at Tranque, years ago, when attached to a whaling voyage, and supplied with a hollow tooth; but, with the assurance of a king, was pleased to be sociable—and they are sociable—I say, I saw Tranquo's great sperm whale, which, after an unusually long raging gale, had been found dead and stranded, with his head against a cocoa-nut tree, whose plumage-like, tufted droopings seemed his verdant jet. When the vast body had at last been stripped of its fathom-deep enfoldings, and the bones become dust dry in the sun, then the skeleton was carefully transported up the Pupella glen, where a grand temple of lordly palms now sheltered it.

But unlike Captain Peleg—who cared not a rush for what are called serious things, and indeed deemed those self-same serious things the veriest of all trifles—Captain Bildad had not only been originally educated according to the strictest sect of Nantucket Quakerism, but all his subsequent ocean life, and the sight of many unclad, lovely island creatures, round the Horn—all that had not moved this native born Quaker one single jot, had not so much as altered one angle of his vest. Still, for all this immutableness, was there some lack of common consistency about worthy Captain Bildad. Though refusing, from conscientious scruples, to bear arms against land invaders, yet himself had illimitably invaded the Atlantic and Pacific; and though a sworn foe to human bloodshed, yet had he in his straight-bodied coat, spilled tuns upon tuns of leviathan gore. How now in the contemplative evening of his days, the pious Bildad reconciled these things in the reminiscence, I do not know; but it did not seem to concern him much, and very probably he had long since come to the sage and sensible conclusion that a man’s religion is one thing, and this practical world quite another. This world pays dividends. Rising from a little cabin-boy in short clothes of the drabbest drab, to a harpooneer in a broad shad-bellied waistcoat; from that becoming boat-header, chief-mate, and captain, and finally a ship owner; Bildad, as I hinted before, had concluded his adventurous career by wholly retiring from active life at the goodly age of sixty, and dedicating his remaining days to the quiet receiving of his well-earned income.

**got:** Like Captain Peleg, Captain Bildad was a well-to-do, retired whaleman. But unlike Captain Peleg—who cared not a rush for what are called serious things, and indeed deemed those self-same serious things the veriest of all trifles—Captain Bildad had been a sworn foe to the frivolities of the world. He was a stern, unyielding man, who had never been known to smile; and though a Quaker, he had been a fighting Quaker, and had fought his way through the world.

What are the sea-rookeries of Brillon, the unappropriated lands of Nantucket, the unexplored interior of Borabora, the untaken kauri timbers, in short what are all these things but so many Loose-Fish? Thus possession is half of the law, and possession is the whole of the law. But often possession is the whole of the law, and in the case of the whale, possession is the whole of the law. And by the eternal Poles! these same Trades that so directly blow my good ship on; these Trades, or something like them—something so unchangeable, and full as strong, blow my keeled soul along! Zogranda, one of their most famous doctors, recommends strips of blubber for infants, as being exceedingly juicy and nourishing. **got:** The fact is, that among his hunters at least, the whale would by all hands be considered a noble dish, were there not so much of him; but when you come to sit down before a whale-steak, you feel as if you were sitting down before a whole ox. **expected:** “Hark ye yet again—the little lower layer. All visible objects, man, are but as pasteboard masks. But in each event—in the living act, the undoubted deed—there, some unknown but still reasoning thing puts forth the mouldings of its features from behind the unreasoning mask. If man will strike, strike through the mask! How can the prisoner reach outside except by thrusting through the wall? To me, the white whale is that wall, shoved near to me. Sometimes I think there’s naught beyond. But ’tis enough. He tasks me; he heaps me; I see in him outrageous strength, with an inscrutable malice sinewing it. That inscrutable thing is chiefly what I hate; and be the white whale agent, or be the white whale principal, I will wreak that hate upon him. Talk not to me of blasphemy, man; I’d strike the sun if it insulted me. For could the sun do that, then could I do the other; since there is ever a sort of fair play herein, jealousy presiding over all creations. But not my master, man, is even that fair play. Who’s over me? Truth hath no confines. Take off thine eye! more intolerable than fiends’ glarings is a doltish stare! So, so; thou reddenest and palest; my heat has melted thee to anger-glow. But look ye, Starbuck, what is said in heat, that thing unsays itself. There are men from whom warm words are small indignity. I meant not to incense thee. Let it go. Look! see yonder Turkish cheeks of spotted tawn—living, breathing pictures painted by the sun. The Pagan leopards—the unrecking and unworshipping things, that live; and seek, and give no reasons for the torrid life they feel! The crew, man, the crew! Are they not one and all with Ahab, in this matter of the whale? See Stubb! he laughs! See yonder Chilian! he snorts to think of it. Stand up amid the general hurricane, thy one tost sapling cannot, Starbuck! And what is it? Reckon it. ’Tis but to help strike a fin; no wondrous feat for Starbuck. What is it more? From this one poor hunt, then, the best lance out of all Nantucket, surely he will not hang back, when every foremast-hand has clutched a whetstone? Ah! constrainings seize thee; I see! the billow lifts thee! Speak, but speak!—Aye, aye! thy silence, then, _that_ voices thee. (_Aside_) Something shot from my dilated nostrils, he has inhaled it in his lungs. Starbuck now is mine; cannot oppose me now, without rebellion.”

**got:** “Hark ye yet again,—the little lower layer. All visible objects, man, are but as pasteboard masks. But in each event—in the living act, the undoubted deed—there, some unknown but still reasoning thing puts forth the mouldings of its features from behind the unreasoning mask. If man will strike, strike through the mask! How can the prisoner reach outside except by thrusting through the wall? To me, the white whale is that wall, shoved near to me. Sometimes I think there’s naught beyond. But ’tis enough. He tasks me; he heaps me; I see in him outrageous strength, with an inscrutable malice sinewing it. That inscrutable thing is chiefly what I hate; and be the white whale agent, or be the white whale principal, I will wreak that hate upon him. Talk not to me of blasphemy, man; I’d strike the sun if it insulted me. For could the sun do that, then could I do the other; since there is ever a sort of fair play herein, jealousy presiding over all creations. But not my master, man, is even that fair play. Who’s over me? Truth hath no confines. Take off thine eye! more intolerable than fiends’ glarings is a doltish stare! ## gemini-3.1-flash-lite k=3 target=1153 score=73.0

**expected:** Now, one of the peculiar characteristics of the savage in his domestic hours, is his wonderful patience of industry. An ancient Hawaiian war-club or spear-paddle, in its full multiplicity and elaboration of carving, is as great a trophy of human perseverance as a Latin lexicon. For, with but a bit of broken sea-shell or a shark’s tooth, that miraculous intricacy of wooden net-work has been achieved; and it has cost steady years of steady application.

**got:** Now, one of the peculiar characteristics of the savage in his domestic hours, is his wonderful patience of industry. An ancient Hawaiian war-club or spear-paddle, in its full multiplicity and elaboration of carving, is as much above the work of a modern cabinet-maker, as in point of mere _art_ the one is above the other. And with this first-rate skrimshander, a sailor will spend months, nay, years, in carving a single tooth, or a single busk, or a single cane-head. And this, too, in the very teeth of the gale, and in the very jaws of the whale. And what is the result? Why, the result is, that the whale-tooth, or the busk, or the cane-head, is a work of art, and a work of art, too, that is not to be sneezed at.

**expected:** But it so happened, that those boats, without seeing Pip, suddenly spying whales close to them on one side, turned, and gave chase; and Stubb’s boat was now so far away, and he and all his crew so intent upon his fish, that Pip’s ringed horizon began to expand around him miserably. By the merest chance the ship itself at last rescued him; but from that hour the little negro went about the deck an idiot; such, at least, they said he was. The sea had jeeringly kept his finite body up, but drowned the infinite of his soul. Not drowned entirely, though. Rather carried down alive to wondrous depths, where strange shapes of the unwarped primal world glided to and fro before his passive eyes; and the miser-merman, Wisdom, revealed his hoarded heaps; and among the joyous, heartless, ever-juvenile eternities, Pip saw the multitudinous, God-omnipresent, coral insects, that out of the firmament of waters heaved the colossal orbs. He saw God’s foot upon the treadle of the loom, and spoke it; and therefore his shipmates called him mad. So man’s insanity is heaven’s sense; and wandering from all mortal reason, man comes at last to that celestial thought, which, to reason, is absurd and frantic; and weal or woe, feels then uncompromised, indifferent as his God.

**got:** But it so happened, that those two boats, without seeing Pip, suddenly spying whales close to them on one side, turned, and gave chase; and Stubb’s boat was now so far away, and he and all his crew so intent upon his fish, that Pip’s ringed horizon began to expand around him miserably. By the merest chance the boat that at last saved him was not the one that had first passed him; but as it was still a long way off, and the sea was running high, and Pip was but a small black speck in the vast blue waste, he was left to the mercy of the waves, and the cruel indifference of the ocean, which, like the world itself, cares nothing for the individual sufferings of its smallest creatures.

**got:** The whale no famous author, and whaling no famous chronicler? Who wrote the first account of our Leviathan? Who but mighty Job! And who composed the first narrative of a whaling-voyage? Who, but no less a man than Thucydides! As for the book-binder’s whale winding like a vine-stalk round the stock of a descending anchor—as stamped and gilded on the backs and title-pages of many books both old and new—that is a mere painter’s flourish, a mere whimsical device of the ancient artists, who, in the headlong, sledging slide of their inspiration, were not always very nice in the natural history of the whale. But passing all this, I have ever found that when narrating to them some specific example of this two-fold enormousness, they have significantly complimented me upon my facetiousness; when, I declare upon my soul, I had no more idea of being facetious than Moses, when he wrote the history of the plagues of Egypt.

**expected:** Assuredly, we must conclude so, if we are to credit the accounts of such gentlemen as Pliny, and the ancient naturalists generally. For Pliny tells us of whales that embraced acres of living bulk, and Aldrovandus of others which measured eight hundred feet in length—Rope Walks and Thames Tunnels of Whales! And even in the days of Banks and Solander, Cooke’s naturalists, we find a Danish member of the Academy of Sciences setting down certain Iceland Whales (reydan-siskur, or Wrinkled Bellies) at one hundred and twenty yards; that is, three hundred and sixty feet. **got:** Assuredly, we must conclude so, if we are to credit the accounts of such gentlemen as Pliny, and the ancient naturalists generally. For Pliny tells us of whales that embraced acres of living bulk, and Aldrovandus of others which measured eight hundred feet in length—Rope Walks and Thames Tunnels of Whales! And even in the days of Banks and Solander, Cook's naturalists, we find a Danish member of the Greenland fishery a century ago, complaining of the scarcity of large whales, a scarcity which, according to this account, must have been still more marked in that fishery's earlier days. Nor must we forget that, in the words of the late Samuel Enderby, merchant of London, "Ten or fifteen years ago the Sperm Whale fishery was almost wholly unknown in the Southern Ocean, and that the Sperm Whale had never been heard of in the North Pacific; whereas now some scores of Green-land whalers are actively engaged in the Pacific Ocean, and Sperm Whales are frequently met with in the North Pacific, and in the Greenland Sea." So that here we have a remarkable instance of a great change in the distribution of a species, which, if it does not argue a positive increase in their numbers, at least argues no decrease.

The three mates now rushed in, and pinioned both his arms behind him; and more rope was brought. Then tying the end of a prolonged rope round his body, they hoisted him up to the main-yard-arm, and there, under a burning sun, with no water near him, they left him to hang, as a warning to the crew. Twice, it is true, the poor wretch essayed to call out for water; but the agony of his wounds, and the violent frenzy of his soul, combined with the cutting agonies of the rope round his loins, stifled all utterance in him; and he hung there, a dying man made dumb by torment."

Therefore he had not solicited a boat’s crew from them, nor had he in any way hinted his desires on that head. Nevertheless he had taken private measures of his own touching all that matter. Until Cabaco’s published discovery, the sailors had little foreseen it, though to be sure when, after being a little while out of port, all hands had concluded the customary business of fitting the whaleboats for service; when some time after this Ahab was now and then found bestirring himself in the matter of making thole-pins with his own hands for what was thought to be one of the spare boats, and even solicitously cutting the small wooden skewers, which when the line is running out are pinned over the groove in the bow: when all this was observed in him, and particularly his solicitude in having an extra coat of sheathing in the bottom of the boat, as if to make it better withstand the pointed pressure of his ivory limb; and also the anxiety he evinced in exactly shaping the thigh board, or clumsy cleat, as it is sometimes called, the horizontal piece in the boat’s bow for bracing the knee against in darting or stabbing at the whale; when it was observed how often he stood up in that boat with his solitary knee fixed in the semi-circular depression in the cleat, and with the carpenter’s chisel gouged out a little here and straightened it a little there; all these things, I say, had awakened much interest and curiosity at the time. But almost everybody supposed that this particular preparative heedfulness in Ahab must only be with a view to the ultimate chase of Moby Dick; for he had already revealed his intention to hunt that mortal monster in person. But such a supposition did by no means involve the remotest suspicion as to any boat’s crew being assigned to that boat.

**got:** Ahab well knew that although his friends at home would think little of his entering a boat in certain comparatively harmless vicissitudes of the chase, for the sake of being near the scene of action and giving his orders to the oarsmen, with the tail of his eye on the chase; yet, in the more perilous vicissitudes of the fishery, they would never consent to his exposing himself in a boat, unless for some all-important and all-absorbing necessity.

The landlord, meanwhile, moved about with a grin that seemed to grow wider with every glass he poured, his eyes darting from one sailor to another as if he were counting his gains in advance. ## gemini-3.1-flash-lite k=10 target=805 score=72.0

**expected:** Though in many natural objects, whiteness refiningly enhances beauty, as if imparting some special virtue of its own, as in marbles, japonicas, and pearls; and though various nations have in some way recognised a certain royal preeminence in this hue; even the barbaric, grand old kings of Pegu placing the title “Lord of the White Elephants” above all their other magniloquent ascriptions of dominion; and the modern kings of Siam unfurling the same snow-white quadruped in the royal standard; and the Hanoverian flag bearing the one figure of a snow-white charger; and the great Austrian Empire, Cæsarian, heir to overlording Rome, having for the imperial colour the same imperial hue; and though this pre-eminence in it applies to the human race itself, giving the white man ideal mastership over every dusky tribe; and though, besides, all this, whiteness has been even made significant of gladness, for among the Romans a white stone marked a joyful day; and though in other mortal sympathies and symbolizings, this same hue is made the emblem of many touching, noble things—the innocence of brides, the benignity of age; though among the Red Men of America the giving of the white belt of wampum was the deepest pledge of honor; though in many climes, whiteness typifies the majesty of Justice in the ermine of the Judge, and contributes to the daily state of kings and queens drawn by milk-white steeds; though even in the higher mysteries of the most august religions it has been made the symbol of the divine spotlessness and power; by the Persian fire worshippers, the white forked flame being held the holiest on the altar; and in the Greek mythologies, Great Jove himself being made incarnate in a snow-white bull; and though to the noble Iroquois, the midwinter sacrifice of the sacred White Dog was by far the holiest festival of their theology, that spotless, faithful creature being held the purest envoy they could send to the Great Spirit with the annual tidings of their own fidelity; and though directly from the Latin word for white, all Christian priests derive the name of one part of their sacred vesture, the alb or tunic, worn beneath the cassock; and though among the holy pomps of the Romish faith, white is specially employed in the celebration of the Passion of our Lord; though in the Vision of St. John, white robes are given to the redeemed, and the four-and-twenty elders stand clothed in white before the great white throne, and the Holy One that sitteth there white like wool; yet for all these accumulated associations, with whatever is sweet, and honorable, and sublime, there yet lurks an elusive something in the innermost idea of this hue, which strikes more of panic to the soul than that redness which affrights in blood.

**got:** Though in many natural objects, whiteness refiningly enhances beauty, as if imparting some special virtue of its own, as in marbles, japonicas, and pearls; and though various nations have in some way recognised a certain royal preeminence in this hue; even befitting their heroic kings and queens; yet for all these accumulated associations, with whatever is sweet, and honourable, and sublime, there yet lurks an elusive something in the innermost idea of this hue, which strikes more of panic to the soul than that redness which affrights in blood.

They soon relinquish this turbulence, however, when they come to be further advanced in life; and then, withdrawing from the herd, they betake themselves to the solitary state of the schoolmaster, or else join some harem as above described. But while these bachelor bulls are yet in their green youth, they are often found in large companies, sometimes amounting to hundreds, and are then known as pods; and it is a curious fact, that though they are so pugnacious among themselves, yet when attacked by man, they will seldom desert each other, but on the contrary, will rally round the wounded one, and strive to protect him with their own bodies.

**got:** At this instant, while Daggoo, on the summit of the head, was clearing the whip—which had somehow got foul of the great cutting tackles—a sharp cracking noise was heard; and to the unspeakable horror of all, one of the two enormous hooks suspending the head tore out of its hold, and the head, with a prodigious commotion, rolled over sideways into the sea, and sank slowly out of sight. But as it went down, the other hook, still holding fast, swung the head round with a vast sweep, so that for an instant the great Heidelburgh Tun was presented mouth upwards, like a huge bowl; and in that instant, by a dexterous heave and toss, Daggoo, with a mighty effort, pitched the bucket, still attached to the whip, right into the orifice, and then, by the help of the men at the windlass, the whole was hoisted up again, and the head, with the bucket in it, was once more suspended in its former position. But poor Tashtego was gone. The head was now a vast cistern of oil, and he was at the bottom of it. The men looked at each other with wild eyes, and for a moment no one spoke. Then, with a sudden impulse, they seized the long pole, and thrust it down into the Tun, in the hope that he might grasp it. But the pole came up again, dripping with oil, and no Tashtego. They tried again and again, but in vain. The oil was too thick, and the man was too far gone. At last, with a heavy heart, they gave up the search, and proceeded to secure the head, which was now a mere shell, the precious contents having been lost in the sea. But as they were about to cut it adrift, a faint gurgling sound was heard from within, and then, to their amazement, a hand appeared at the orifice, grasping the edge of the Tun. It was Tashtego, who, by a desperate effort, had managed to climb up the side of the cistern, and was now clinging to the rim, half dead with exhaustion and suffocation. With a shout of joy, the men hauled him out, and he was soon restored to consciousness. But the oil was lost, and the head was a wreck. The Pequod had to content herself with the few tubs that had been filled before the accident, and to sail away with a heavy heart, leaving the great Heidelburgh Tun to sink into the depths of the sea.

The pale, deadly-looking shark, that swims in the wake of the ship, and the polar bear, that stalks over the ice-fields, are the most dreaded of all creatures. And when we consider that other theory of the natural philosophers, that all other earthly colors—hue melting in hue—are but subtile deceits, not actually inherent in substances, but only laid on from without; so that all deified Nature absolutely paints like the harlot, whose allurements cover nothing but the charnel-house within; and when we proceed further, and consider that the mystical cosmetic which produces every one of her hues, the great principle of light, for ever remains white or colorless in itself, and if operating without medium upon matter, would touch all objects, even tulips and roses, with its own blank tinge—pondering all this, the palsied universe lies before us a leper; and like wilful travellers in Lapland, who refuse to wear colored and coloring glasses upon their eyes, so the wretched infidel gazes himself blind at the monumental white shroud that wraps all the prospect around him. His eyes, or rather the places where his eyes had been, were beheld. As strange misgrown masses gather in the knot-holes of the noblest oaks when prostrate, so from the points which the whale's eyes had once occupied, now protruded blind bulbs, horribly pitiable to see. But pity there was none. For all his old age, and his one arm, and his blind eyes, he must die the death and be murdered, in order to light the gay bridals and other merry-makings of men, and also to illuminate the solemn churches that preach unconditional inoffensiveness by all to all.

## claude-haiku-4.5 k=3 target=1560 score=71.0

**expected:** The gallant Perseus, a son of Jupiter, was the first whaleman; and to the eternal honor of our calling be it said, that the first whale attacked by our brotherhood was not killed with any sordid intent. Every one knows the fine story of Perseus and Andromeda; how the lovely Andromeda, the daughter of a king, was tied to a rock on the sea-coast, and as Leviathan was in the very act of carrying her off, Perseus, the prince of whalemen, intrepidly advancing, harpooned the monster, and delivered and married the maid. It was an admirable artistic exploit, rarely achieved by the best harpooneers of the present day; inasmuch as this Leviathan was slain at the very first dart. And let no man doubt this Arkite story; for in the ancient Joppa, now Jaffa, on the Syrian coast, in one of the Pagan temples, there stood for many ages the vast skeleton of a whale, which the city’s legends and all the inhabitants asserted to be the identical bones of the monster that Perseus slew. When the Romans took Joppa, the same skeleton was carried to Italy in triumph. What seems most singular and suggestively important in this story, is this: it was from Joppa that Jonah set sail.

**got:** The gallant Perseus, a son of Jupiter, was the first whaleman; and to the eternal honor of our calling be it said, that the first whale attacked by our brotherhood was not killed with any sordid intent. Those were the knightly days of our profession, when we only killed our monsters in order to kill some other monsters that preyed upon mankind. But those fine times have long since passed away; for the world has scarcely heard of the great Leviathan, except in the most sordid and mercenary connexion with the blubber trade. Yet call me unpractical if you will; I stand by the profession.

"It is the common usage, all the world over," he said. "The lay, or share of the profits, is the common pay of the whaleman. It is the share of the profits, and that alone. ## claude-haiku-4.5 k=1 target=1154 score=71.0

**expected:** As with the Hawaiian savage, so with the white sailor-savage. With the same marvellous patience, and with the same single shark’s tooth, of his one poor jack-knife, he will carve you a bit of bone sculpture, not quite as workmanlike, but as close packed in its maziness of design, as the Greek savage, Achilles’s shield; and full of barbaric spirit and suggestiveness, as the prints of that fine old Dutch savage, Albert Durer.

**got:** As with the Hawaiian savage, so with the white sailor-savage. With the same marvellous patience, and with the same single shark's tooth, of his one poor jack-knife, he will carve you a bit of scrimshaw, as an American whale-ship, in miniature, complete with all its spars and tackle. Long ere civilisation reached the Hawaiian islands, the lonely and scattered crews of American whalers were practising upon the very bone and tooth of their huge catches, a carving as intricate and elaborate as the work of any civilised artisan. Nay, I have known a whale-ship's carpenter, of my acquaintance, to take a bit of the jaws of his well-beloved leviathan, and fashioning it into all manner of boxes and miniature vessels, spend whole nights in carving the most delicate designs upon them, with no tool but a jack-knife, and the point of a sail-needle. Such is the wonderful patience and industry of the sailor in his hours of ease.

## claude-haiku-4.5 k=3 target=1154 score=70.0

**expected:** As with the Hawaiian savage, so with the white sailor-savage. With the same marvellous patience, and with the same single shark’s tooth, of his one poor jack-knife, he will carve you a bit of bone sculpture, not quite as workmanlike, but as close packed in its maziness of design, as the Greek savage, Achilles’s shield; and full of barbaric spirit and suggestiveness, as the prints of that fine old Dutch savage, Albert Durer.

**got:** As with the Hawaiian savage, so with the white sailor-savage. With the same marvellous patience, and with the same single shark's tooth, of his one jack-knife, he will carve you a bit of bone or tooth so elaborately and so wonderfully wrought, that it shall have taken him months of deliberate labour, and fitted him for a king's cabinet. Scrimshander is the name which the whaleman bestows upon this art of his; and an ardent devotee to it I have long been, myself. Nor is there anything in it unworthy the while; for be the carver who he may, poor or rich, educated or ignorant, if he has but the patience, the cunning, and the love, he can fashion forth a bit of bone into a miracle of human handicraft.

## claude-sonnet-4.6 k=10 target=50 score=70.0

**expected:** I observed, however, that one of them held somewhat aloof, and though he seemed desirous not to spoil the hilarity of his shipmates by his own sober face, yet upon the whole he refrained from making as much noise as the rest. This man interested me at once; and since the sea-gods had ordained that he should soon become my shipmate (though but a sleeping-partner one, so far as this narrative is concerned), I will here venture upon a little description of him. He stood full six feet in height, with noble shoulders, and a chest like a coffer-dam. I have seldom seen such brawn in a man. His face was deeply brown and burnt, making his white teeth dazzling by the contrast; while in the deep shadows of his eyes floated some reminiscences that did not seem to give him much joy. His voice at once announced that he was a Southerner, and from his fine stature, I thought he must be one of those tall mountaineers from the Alleghanian Ridge in Virginia. When the revelry of his companions had mounted to its height, this man slipped away unobserved, and I saw no more of him till he became my comrade on the sea. In a few minutes, however, he was missed by his shipmates, and being, it seems, for some reason a huge favourite with them, they raised a cry of “Bulkington! Bulkington! where’s Bulkington?” and darted out of the house in pursuit of him.

**got:** I observed, however, that one of them held somewhat aloof, and though he seemed desirous not to spoil the hilarity of his shipmates by his own sober face, yet upon the whole he refrained from making as much noise as the rest. This man interested me at once; and since the sea-gods had ordained that he should soon become my shipmate (though but a sleeping-partner of mine, so to speak), I thought it best, not having yet been introduced to him, to keep a sharp look-out upon him, while yet all aback with wonder at the rest. The stranger's eye was now upon me; and as I stood there, I could not help thinking that if ever I should give battle to Moby Dick, and survive it, or if I should be so lucky as to escape from it with only one arm, why, in that case, I might as well begin by telling him something of my previous experiences.

Meanwhile, others of the ship’s company were tumultuously busy at the masonry of the try-works, from which the huge pots had been removed. **got:** As this glad ship of good luck bore down upon the moody Pequod, the barbarian sound of enormous drums came from her forecastle; and drawing still nearer, a crowd of her men were seen standing round her huge try-pots, which, covered with the parchment-like _skins_ of the sperm whale, contained, as with the scalding pots of the cannibals, the seething contents of a newly killed whale; and the watch were employed in ladling out the oil into the tubs, and in the midst of this fragrant job, they were singing away at a rate which seemed to imply that they thought their ship was the very last word in jollity.

## gemini-3.1-flash-lite k=10 target=801 score=70.0

**expected:** Here, then, was this grey-headed, ungodly old man, chasing with curses a Job’s whale round the world, at the head of a crew, too, chiefly made up of mongrel renegades, and castaways, and cannibals—morally enfeebled also, by the incompetence of mere unaided virtue or right-mindedness in Starbuck, the invulnerable jollity of indifference and recklessness in Stubb, and the pervading mediocrity in Flask. Such a crew, so officered, seemed specially picked and packed by some infernal fatality to help him to his monomaniac revenge. How it was that they so aboundingly responded to the old man’s ire—by what evil magic their souls were possessed, that at times his hate seemed almost theirs; the White Whale as much their insufferable foe as his; how all this came to be—what the White Whale was to them, or how to their unconscious understandings, also, in some dim, unsuspected way, he might have seemed the gliding great demon of the seas of life,—all this to explain, would be to dive deeper than Ishmael can go. The subterranean miner that works in us all, how can one tell whither leads his shaft by the ever shifting, muffled sound of his pick? Who does not feel the irresistible arm drag? What skiff in tow of a seventy-four can stand still? For one, I gave myself up to the abandonment of the time and the place; but while yet all a-rush to encounter the whale, could see naught in that brute but the deadliest ill.

**got:** Here, then, was this grey-headed, ungodly old man, chasing with curses a Job’s whale round the world, at the head of a crew, too, chiefly made up of mongrel renegades, and castaways, and cannibals—morally enfeebled also, by the incompetence of mere unaided virtue or right-mindedness in Starbuck, the first mate: first mate, as I have said, to Ahab, whose whole high-wrought character, by a process of that, which, in the case of the whale, I have called the monomaniac incarnation, had been so thoroughly fused into the whale, that the whale was now the object of his supreme hate, and the only thing in the world that he could not, or would not, forgive.

## kimi-k3 k=10 target=1138 score=70.0

**expected:** But it may be fancied, that from the naked skeleton of the stranded whale, accurate hints may be derived touching his true form. Not at all. For it is one of the more curious things about this Leviathan, that his skeleton gives very little idea of his general shape. Though Jeremy Bentham’s skeleton, which hangs for candelabra in the library of one of his executors, correctly conveys the idea of a burly-browed utilitarian old gentleman, with all Jeremy’s other leading personal characteristics; yet nothing of this kind could be inferred from any leviathan’s articulated bones. In fact, as the great Hunter says, the mere skeleton of the whale bears the same relation to the fully invested and padded animal as the insect does to the chrysalis that so roundingly envelopes it. This peculiarity is strikingly evinced in the head, as in some part of this book will be incidentally shown. It is also very curiously displayed in the side fin, the bones of which almost exactly answer to the bones of the human hand, minus only the thumb. This fin has four regular bone-fingers, the index, middle, ring, and little finger. But all these are permanently lodged in their fleshy covering, as the human fingers in an artificial covering. “However recklessly the whale may sometimes serve us,” said humorous Stubb one day, “he can never be truly said to handle us without mittens.”

**got:** But it may be fancied, that from the naked skeleton of the stranded whale, accurate hints may be derived touching his true form. Not at all. For it is one of the more curious things about this Leviathan, that his skeleton gives very little idea of his general shape. Though Jeremy Bentham’s skeleton, which hangs for candelabra in the library of one of his executors, correctly conveys the idea of a burly-browed utilitarian old gentleman, with all Jeremy’s other leading personal characteristics; yet nothing of this kind will be found in the skeleton of a whale, which is but a sort of huge bony kite, with a few ribs on one side, and a long, straight, bony ridge on the other, and a tail like a weaver’s beam. The skeleton dimensions I shall now proceed to set down are copied verbatim from my right arm, where I had them tattooed; as in my wild wanderings at that period, there was no other secure way of preserving such valuable statistics. But as I was crowded for space, and wished the other parts of my body to remain a blank page for a poem I was then composing—at least, what untattooed parts might remain—I did not trouble myself with the odd inches; nor, indeed, should inches at all enter into a congenial admeasurement of the whale.

Come in thy lowest form of love, and I will kneel and kiss thee; but at thy highest, come as mere supernal power; and though thou launchest navies of full-freighted worlds, there’s that in here that still remains indifferent. **got:** “Oh! thou clear spirit of clear fire, whom on these seas I as Persian once did worship, till in the sacramental act so burned by thee, that to this hour I bear the scar; I now know thee, thou clear spirit, and I now know that thy right worship is defiance. To neither love nor reverence wilt thou be kind; and e’en for hate thou canst but kill; and all are killed. No fearless fool now fronts thee. I own thy speechless, placeless power; said I not so? Nor was it wrung from me; nor do I now drop these links. Thou canst blind; but I can then grope. Thou canst consume; but I can then be ashes. But the evil, the fire, is even in thee, and thou art but the instrument. I am the Fates’ lieutenant; I act under orders; look thou to it. Swerve me? The path to my fixed purpose is laid with iron rails, whereon my soul is grooved to run. Over unsounded gorges, through the rifled hearts of mountains, under torrents’ beds, unerringly I rush! Naught’s an obstacle, naught’s an angle to the iron way!”

With a grating rush, the three lines flew round the loggerheads with such a force as to gouge deep grooves in them; while so fearful were the harpooneers that this rapid sounding would soon exhaust the lines, that using all their dexterous might, they caught repeated smoking turns with the rope to hold on; till at last—owing to the perpendicular strain from the lead-lined chocks of the boats, whence the three ropes went straight down into the blue—the gunwales of the bows were almost even with the water, while the three sterns tilted high in the air. And the whale soon ceasing to sound, for some time they remained in that attitude, fearful of expending more line, though the position was a little ticklish. But though boats have been taken down and lost in this way, yet it is this “holding on,” as it is called; this hooking up by the sharp barbs of his live flesh from the back; this it is that often torments the Leviathan into soon rising again to meet the sharp lance of his foes. Yet not to speak of the peril of the thing, it is to be doubted whether this course is always the best; for it is but reasonable to presume, that the longer the stricken whale stays under water, the more he is exhausted. Because, owing to the enormous surface of him—in a full grown sperm whale something less than 2000 square feet—the pressure of the water is immense. We all know what an astonishing atmospheric weight we ourselves stand up under; even here, above-ground, in the air; how vast, then, the burden of a whale, bearing on his back a column of two hundred fathoms of ocean! It must at least equal the weight of fifty atmospheres. One whaleman has estimated it at the weight of twenty line-of-battle ships, with all their guns, and stores, and men on board.

## qwen3.7-max k=10 target=509 score=69.0

**expected:** _The whale no famous author, and whaling no famous chronicler?_ Who wrote the first account of our Leviathan? Who but mighty Job! And who composed the first narrative of a whaling-voyage? **got:** The whale has no famous author, and whaling no famous chronicler, you will say. But who wrote the first account of the Leviathan? Who but Job! And who composed the first narrative of a whaling voyage? Who but Jonah! And who was the first to record the wonders of the deep? Who but the inspired Psalmist! Nor is it only in the sacred writings that the whale and his hunter are enshrined; for in all profane literature, from the earliest times down to the present day, they have been celebrated by the greatest poets and philosophers. Homer sang of them; Aristotle described them; Pliny discoursed upon them; and in modern times, Burke, Kant, and Goethe have not disdained to make them the subjects of their profoundest speculations. So that if the whale and whaling have no famous authors or chroniclers, it is not because they are unworthy of such distinction, but because they are too vast and too wonderful to be adequately set forth by any human pen.

## claude-sonnet-4.6 k=10 target=1606 score=68.0

**expected:** Fourth: Stealing unawares upon the whale in the fancied security of the middle of solitary seas, you find him unbent from the vast corpulence of his dignity, and kitten-like, he plays on the ocean as if it were a hearth. But still you see his power in his play. The broad palms of his tail are flirted high into the air; then smiting the surface, the thunderous concussion resounds for miles. You would almost think a great gun had been discharged; and if you noticed the light wreath of vapor from the spiracle at his other extremity, you would think that that was the smoke from the touch-hole.

**got:** Fourth: Stealing unawares upon the whale in the fancied security of the middle of solitary seas, you find him unbent from the vast corpulence of his dignity, and kitten-like, he plays on the ocean as if it were a hearth. But still another inquiry remains; one often agitated by the more recondite Nantucketers. Whether owing to the almost omniscient look-outs at the mast-heads of the whale-ships, now penetrating even through Behring's straits, and into the remotest secret drawers and lockers of the world; and the thousand harpoons and lances darted along all continental coasts; the moot point is, whether Leviathan can long endure so wide a chase, and so remorseless a havoc; whether he must not at last be exterminated from the waters, and the last whale, like the last man, smoke his last pipe, and then himself evaporate in the final puff.

## claude-sonnet-4.6 k=1 target=1560 score=68.0

**expected:** The gallant Perseus, a son of Jupiter, was the first whaleman; and to the eternal honor of our calling be it said, that the first whale attacked by our brotherhood was not killed with any sordid intent. Those were the knightly days of our profession, when we only bore arms to succor the distressed, and not to fill men’s lamp-feeders. Every one knows the fine story of Perseus and Andromeda; how the lovely Andromeda, the daughter of a king, was tied to a rock on the sea-coast, and as Leviathan was in the very act of carrying her off, Perseus, the prince of whalemen, intrepidly advancing, harpooned the monster, and delivered and married the maid. It was an admirable artistic exploit, rarely achieved by the best harpooneers of the present day; inasmuch as this Leviathan was slain at the very first dart. And let no man doubt this Arkite story; for in the ancient Joppa, now Jaffa, on the Syrian coast, in one of the Pagan temples, there stood for many ages the vast skeleton of a whale, which the city’s legends and all the inhabitants asserted to be the identical bones of the monster that Perseus slew. When the Romans took Joppa, the same skeleton was carried to Italy in triumph. What seems most singular and suggestively important in this story, is this: it was from Joppa that Jonah set sail.

**got:** The gallant Perseus, a son of Jupiter, was the first whaleman; and to the eternal honor of our calling be it said, that the first whale attacked by our brotherhood was not killed with a harpoon, but with a sword; and the fish received not the customary summons to surrender; and thereupon the mariners came to the rescue, and, striking into him with their lances, and drag him up to the ship's side, where he was quickly dispatched, and the great monster was killed.

Through thee, thy flaming self, my scorched eyes do dimly see it. Oh, thou foundling fire, thou hermit immemorial, thou too hast thy incommunicable riddle, thy unparticipated grief. **expected:** I know that this queer adventure of the Gay-Header’s will be sure to seem incredible to some landsmen, though they themselves may have either seen or heard of some one’s falling into a cistern ashore; an accident which not seldom happens, and with much less reason too than the Indian’s, considering the exceeding slipperiness of the curb of the Sperm Whale’s well.

**got:** I know that this queer adventure of the Gay-Header’s will be sure to seem incredible to some landsmen, though they themselves may have been somewhat acquainted with the perilous predicament of the whale-fishery; but let them know that this is no fable. The sperm whale-head, as before described, is a vast, hollow, bell-shaped thing, and when the whale is dead, it is a very easy matter to dive into it, and even to remain there for a short time, provided you can hold your breath. But the danger is, that the whale may be so far gone in decomposition, that the head may be ready to burst, or that the diver may be suffocated by the foul air within. In this case, however, the whale was fresh, and the head was as sound as a bell, so that the only danger was from the pressure of the water, and the difficulty of getting out again.

## claude-haiku-4.5 k=1 target=1560 score=67.0

**expected:** The gallant Perseus, a son of Jupiter, was the first whaleman; and to the eternal honor of our calling be it said, that the first whale attacked by our brotherhood was not killed with any sordid intent. Those were the knightly days of our profession, when we only bore arms to succor the distressed, and not to fill men’s lamp-feeders. Every one knows the fine story of Perseus and Andromeda; how the lovely Andromeda, the daughter of a king, was tied to a rock on the sea-coast, and as Leviathan was in the very act of carrying her off, Perseus, the prince of whalemen, intrepidly advancing, harpooned the monster, and delivered and married the maid. It was an admirable artistic exploit, rarely achieved by the best harpooneers of the present day; inasmuch as this Leviathan was slain at the very first dart. And let no man doubt this Arkite story; for in the ancient Joppa, now Jaffa, on the Syrian coast, in one of the Pagan temples, there stood for many ages the vast skeleton of a whale, which the city’s legends and all the inhabitants asserted to be the identical bones of the monster that Perseus slew. When the Romans took Joppa, the same skeleton was carried to Italy in triumph. What seems most singular and suggestively important in this story, is this: it was from Joppa that Jonah set sail.

**got:** The gallant Perseus, a son of Jupiter, was the first whaleman; and to the eternal honor of our calling, be it said, that the first whale attacked by our brotherhood was not killed with any sordid intent. Those were the knightly days of our profession, when we only slew the Leviathan from a sense of religious duty. That was certainly different and far more honorable than the current state of whaling, I am sure. I try not to think of the days when the business of whaling was a thing of honor and religious significance, and men pursued it as they would a holy calling. But even now, even in these days of ours, the whale-fishery is ennobled by the fact that it has been followed by the noblest of mankind.

Hearing no answer, Stubb turned to the Frenchman again. "Well, never mind about that now. Let's have a look at your ambergris. Where is it?" "There," said the Frenchman, pointing with his lance. But Stubb saw nothing but a continuation of the same blubber; so he concluded that either the Frenchman was humbugging him, or that he himself did not have the proper eye for ambergris in the blubber.

But Queequeg himself seemed to regard the matter with perfect indifference, as if the coffin were the most natural thing in the world, and as if he had been expecting it all along. Indeed, when the carpenter had finished his work and brought the coffin to him, Queequeg merely glanced at it, and nodded his head in a manner which seemed to express his entire satisfaction with the workmanship. Then he quietly lay down in it, to try how comfortable it was, stretching himself out at full length, and adjusting his position, as if he were merely lying down in his hammock for an afternoon nap.

**got:** This elusive quality it is, which causes the thought of whiteness, when divorced from more kindly associations, and coupled with any object terrible in itself, to heighten that terror to the furthest bounds. We will now cite one or two instances, and then proceed to the more special considerations which are immediately connected with the theme of this chapter. The native American Indians, as is well known, were wont to carve their pipes out of a white stone, which they called "the Indian's pipe," and which was held in great reverence among them; but the mere accident of the whiteness of the stone, and not its carver's skill, was the cause of the veneration. The white bear of the polar regions, and the white shark of the tropics, are creatures that have been regarded with a horror superstitious and intense; and the albatross, that white phantom of the southern seas, has been the cause of many a dark superstition among sailors.

It was a long, narrow, and deep chest, and the carpenter, after some little trouble, managed to fit it with a ring-bolt and a rope, so that it could be easily attached to the stern. It was a strange sight to see the coffin of the living man being prepared for the use of the dead, or rather, for the use of those who might yet be living, and it was a sight that made even the most hardened of the crew feel a strange, cold shiver run down their spines. But Queequeg himself seemed to take it all as a matter of course, and even helped the carpenter with his work, as if he were preparing a bed for himself to sleep in, rather than a life-buoy for others to cling to in their hour of need.

But I have no time for such celestial matters now; for though I might expatiate upon the zodiacal Leviathan, yet my present business is with the terrestrial one, and I must hasten to show that even in the most practical and matter-of-fact view, whaling is not without its own peculiar and inherent dignity.

This whale is not dead; he is only dispirited; out of sorts, perhaps; hypochondriac; and so supine, that the hinges of his jaw have relaxed, leaving him there in that ungainly sort of plight, a reproach to all his tribe, who must, no doubt, imprecate lock-jaws upon him.

**got:** But come out now, and look at this portentous lower jaw, which seems like the long narrow lid of an immense snuff-box, with the hinge at one end, instead of one side. If you pry it up, so as to get a good view of its interior, you will find that on the under side it is lined with a similar membrane to that of the upper part; but on the upper side, where the teeth are inserted, it is covered with a thick, fleshy substance, in which the teeth are embedded, and which, when the whale is alive, is of a bright vermilion color.

**got:** It may be but an idle whim, but it has always seemed to me, that the extraordinary vacillations of movement displayed by some whales when transfixed by three or four harpoons; as when, for instance, a whale, after receiving three harpoons in his life-line, has been known to run out upwards of three hundred and fifty yards of line; irregularly curving hither and thither by sheer dint of his vertical flukes, all the while churning the water into foam, as if that violent and disorderly manner of motion were the result of intense agony;—I say it may be but an idle whim, but I have thought that all this might be owing to the wondrous fact, that when the whale's brain is illuminated by the extraordinary concourse of impressions brought to it by the thousand avenues of perception, it might be that he is, for the time being, as much confused and perplexed as a man might be, were he suddenly surrounded by the clamorous voices of a thousand orators, all shouting at him simultaneously in a thousand different languages.

## claude-haiku-4.5 k=10 target=823 score=65.0

**expected:** Nor is it, altogether, the remembrance of her cathedral-toppling earthquakes; nor the stampedoes of her frantic seas; nor the tearlessness of arid skies that never rain; nor the sight of her wide field of leaning spires, wrenched cope-stones, and crosses all adroop (like canted yards of anchored fleets); and her suburban avenues of house-walls lying over upon each other, as a tossed pack of cards;—it is not these things alone which make tearless Lima, the strangest, saddest city thou can’st see. For Lima has taken the white veil; and there is a higher horror in this whiteness of her woe. Old as Pizarro, this whiteness keeps her ruins for ever new; admits not the cheerful greenness of complete decay; spreads over her broken ramparts the rigid pallor of an apoplexy that fixes its own distortions.

**got:** Nor is it, altogether, the remembrance of her cathedral-toppling earthquakes; nor the stampedoes of her frantic seas; nor the tearlessness of arid skies that never rain; nor the sight of her wide field of leaning spires, wrenched cope-stones, and crosses all adroop (like jaded javelins of spent invaders back to camp); which, as a nation, spell out the desolation of Peru. Rather is it, that when we stand before the wreck of that immemorial city, and think of what it was, and of the mightiness of the quelled Andes that hem it in, some vast, indefinite dread of the "hereafter" steals over us; a dread of matter; a dread of Deity; methinks, a dread of the White Whale of the Pacific and Indian Oceans, that most monstrous and most mountainous! For there is no folly of the beasts of the earth which is not infinitely outdone by the madness of men. But, be all this as it may, let us try to unwind these riddles. It may be that by thus persistently contemplating them, by repeatedly invoking and re-invoking them, and so repeatedly hammering them and the like into us, we may hammer into ourselves some certain significance lurking in all things, else we come to doubt, whether there be any significance in anything.

## claude-haiku-4.5 k=1 target=1444 score=65.0

**expected:** It may be but an idle whim, but it has always seemed to me, that the extraordinary vacillations of movement displayed by some whales when beset by three or four boats; the timidity and liability to queer frights, so common to such whales; I think that all this indirectly proceeds from the helpless perplexity of volition, in which their divided and diametrically opposite powers of vision must involve them.

**got:** It may be but an idle whim, but it has always seemed to me, that the extraordinary vacillations of movement displayed by some whales when chased, that inconsistency of conduct which I have elsewhere spoken of as evincing their bewilderment and indecision, may be imputed to the following cause: that in the pursuit of the whale, the boats get as close as possible to the whale's side, and the whale, wheeling about, perceives the boats on one side of him, in an unobstructed line of sight, and also by some manner of refraction sees the boats in precisely the opposite direction, on his other side. Being thus, as it were, locked and loaded on both sides, with a pair of boats before him and a pair behind him, all four boats in his immediate vicinity, the whale becomes bewildered and knows not which way to turn; and this bewilderment is evinced by his aforesaid vacillations of movement, wherein he seems neither to advance nor to retreat, but to mill about in a state of indecision and perplexity.
